Initially she coped by doubling down on her usual meticulous routines—late nights refining CAD models, weekend troubleshooting of 3-D printed components, and volunteering for extra tasks to prove herself. Over time, the workload and a perfectionistic internal standard eroded her boundaries: she stopped running with her track club, skipped board-game nights, and began spending most evenings alone in her apartment reviewing work in her head instead of resting. When she started to notice that she was no longer enjoying her technical hobbies and that she was making small but consequential mistakes at work because she could not focus on even simple schematics, her manager suggested the employee assistance program, which led to a psychiatric referral.\n- Chief Complaint: “I used to be able to focus for hours on designs, but now my mind feels foggy and I can’t make even basic decisions at work.”\n▸ Patient-specific Symptom Manifestations\n- S019 (Delusions): I’ve become convinced that the mistakes in my CAD files have set off a chain of failures on the project, and that my boss and the client are quietly gathering evidence to hold me personally responsible when something catastrophic eventually happens, even though people keep telling me the errors were minor and already fixed.\n- S020 (Hallucinations): Every so often when I’m alone in my apartment or sitting at my desk late, I hear this calm, almost clinical voice in my head telling me things like, “You ruined the project, you don’t deserve this job, you should be punished,” and it sounds so real that I find myself looking around to see who said it.\n- S025 (Loss_of_Interest): I used to get genuinely excited about tinkering with 3-D printers or going on long runs with my track group, but for months now those things just feel flat and pointless, and I end up sitting on my forever, I just stare at parts before picking them up, and even coworkers have asked if I’m okay because I move and react so slowly in the shop.\n- S030 (Worthlessness_Guilt): Most days I feel like a complete fraud who slipped through the hiring process, and I lie awake replaying every tiny oversight at work, convincing myself that I don’t deserve my degree or my job and that everyone would be better off if I weren’t dragging the team down.\n- S031 (Diminished_Thinking): Where I used to be able to zone in on a design problem for hours, now my brain feels like it’s full of static—I reread the same line on a drawing five times, can’t decide something as simple as which tolerance scheme to use, and even picking what to eat for dinner can paralyze me for fifteen minutes.\n- S032 (Death_Suicide): On bad nights, when I’m lying there thinking about how I’ve supposedly ruined everything, I catch myself wishing I just wouldn’t wake up, and I’ve even found myself picturing in detail what it would be like to drive my car off the overpass on my commute, even though I haven’t acted on it.\n▸ Additional Diagnostic Requirements\n- delusions_and_hallucinations_occur_exclusively_during_the_major_depressive_episode: Those thoughts that my boss is secretly building a case against me and the harsh voice telling me I should be punished only started after I slid into this foggy, joyless state last year, and on the rare days when my mood and energy feel closer to normal, that paranoid feeling and the voice pretty much disappear.\n- functional_impairment: Over the past year I’ve gone from being one of the more reliable people on the team to missing internal deadlines, needing coworkers to double-check simple drawings, and turning down almost all social plans because I’m either too exhausted or too wrapped up in worrying about work to function normally outside the job.\n- duration: Ever since a few months after I started the full-time position on this aerospace project—so for a little over a year now—I’ve felt this heavy depression with the guilt, foggy thinking, and scary thoughts about being to blame disappear.\n- functional_impairment: Over the past year I’ve gone from being one of the more reliable people on the team to missing internal deadlines, needing coworkers to double-check simple detail what it would be like to drive my car off the overpass on my commute, even though I haven’t acted on it.\n▸ Additional Diagnostic Requirements\n- My manager eventually noticed how much I was struggling with the stress and suggested the employee assistance program, which is what led to this referral.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me more about how this foggy feeling and difficulty making decisions started, was it sudden or did it come on gradually, and have you experienced any significant stressful events recently?\n\nPatient: It came on gradually after I transitioned to a full-time engineering technician role on a high-pressure aerospace project about fourteen months ago, which had tight deadlines and a demanding supervisor. I tried to cope by working longer hours, but the constant stress eventually wore me down until my brain felt full of static. Now, making even basic decisions at work or just deciding what to eat for dinner completely paralyzes me.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed a decrease in interest or pleasure in activities you used to enjoy, such as designing, since you started feeling this way?\n\nPatient: Yes, I've completely lost interest in the things I used to love. Activities like tinkering with 3-D printers, running with my track club, or playing board games just feel flat and pointless now. Instead of doing any of that, I usually just end up sitting on my couch scrolling through old work emails.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself feeling guilty or worthless about your inability to perform at work or enjoy the activities you used to love?\n\nPatient: Yes, I constantly feel like a complete fraud who shouldn't have been hired, and I lie awake replaying tiny work mistakes, feeling like I don't even deserve my degree. Not being able to enjoy running or 3-D printing anymore makes me feel even more useless, like I'm just wasting away on the couch.\n\nDoctor: Can you describe a specific situation recently where you felt overwhelmed by these feelings of guilt or worthlessness, and how you responded to them?\n\nPatient: The other night, I lay awake for hours replaying a tiny dimension error I made in a CAD file, feeling like a complete fraud who doesn't even deserve my degree. I was convinced I was dragging the entire team down with my incompetence, so I just lay there paralyzed in the dark, overwhelmed by the feeling that everyone would be better off without me.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any traumatic events, either recently or in the past, that might be contributing to your feelings of guilt, worthlessness, or being overwhelmed?\n\nPatient: No, I can't think of any specific traumatic events in my past or recently. I think my feelings of guilt and being overwhelmed really stem from the intense pressure, the demanding supervisor, and the heavy workload of transitioning into this full-time aerospace engineering role.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned that these feelings started about fourteen months ago, but can you tell me if there was a period where your symptoms significantly worsened or if they have been steadily increasing over this time?\n\nPatient: They have been steadily increasing and getting worse over time rather than spiking suddenly. At first, I tried to cope by working harder, but over the months my boundaries eroded, I stopped my hobbies, and the cognitive fog set in. It was only after I had already slid into this heavy, joyless state that the paranoid thoughts and the voice in my head eventually started.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned that your symptoms have been steadily increasing over the past fourteen months, but can you tell me if there was a distinct period, even if brief, where you felt relatively better or if the worsening has been continuous without any significant improvements?\n\nPatient: It has been pretty constant, and this heavy depression, guilt, and foggy thinking have hung over me most days for a little over a year. However, on the very rare days when my mood and energy feel a bit closer to normal, those paranoid feelings and the voice in my head pretty much disappear. It is only during those brief moments that I get any kind of break from it.\n\nDoctor: You've mentioned that your symptoms have been steadily worsening over the past fourteen months, but can you clarify if there was ever a two-week period or more during this time where you felt significantly better, or has the depression been persistent without any notable periods of improvement?\n\nPatient: No, there has never been a two-week period where I felt significantly better or back to normal. The heavy fog and guilt have been persistent almost every single day for over a year now. I only get very rare, isolated days where my mood feels slightly closer to normal, but otherwise it has been continuous.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned that your symptoms have been persistent for over a year, but can you confirm if you've had any periods, no matter how brief, where you felt completely back to your normal self, without any depressive symptoms, for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: No, I can definitely confirm I haven't had any two-week periods where I felt completely back to normal. This heavy depression has been constant for over a year now, with no real break from the symptoms. Even on the rare single day where I feel slightly closer to my usual self, it never lasts and I don't feel fully recovered.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any periods of elevated mood, increased energy, or decreased need for sleep that lasted for several days or more at any point in the past fourteen months?\n\nPatient: No, I’ve never experienced anything like that—no periods of high energy, elevated mood, or needing less sleep. On the rare days when I feel a bit better, my energy and mood just feel closer to my normal baseline. I certainly don't feel hyper, high, or overly energetic.\n\nDoctor: Can you confirm if you have experienced two or more major depressive episodes in the past, with at least two months of recovery in between, or has this been a single continuous episode?"
  }

This has been a single, continuous episode. It started a little over a year ago, a few months after I transitioned into the full-time aerospace position, and it has been ongoing every day since. I haven't had any periods of recovery or felt like my old self at all during this time.

her manager suggested the employee assistance program, which led to a psychiatric referral.\n- Chief Complaint: “I used to be able to focus for hours on designs, but now my mind feels foggy and I can’t make even basic decisions at work.”\n▸ Patient-specific Symptom Manifestations\n- S019 (Delusions): I’ve become convinced that the mistakes in my CAD files have set off a chain of failures on the project, and that my boss and the client are quietly gathering evidence to hold me personally responsible when something catastrophic eventually happens, even though people keep telling me the errors were minor and already fixed.\n- S020 (Hallucinations): Every so often when I’m alone in my apartment or sitting at my desk late, I hear this calm, almost clinical voice in my head telling me things like, “You ruined the project, you don’t deserve this job, you should be punished,” and it sounds so real that I find myself looking around to see who said it.\n- S025 (Loss_of_Interest): I used to get genuinely excited about tinkering with 3-D printers or going on long runs with my track group, but for months now those things just feel flat and pointless, and I end up sitting on my couch scrolling through old emails instead of doing anything I used to like.\n- S028 (Psychomotor_Changes): Lately it feels like my body is moving through molasses—walking from my car to the lab takes forever, I just stare at parts before picking them up, and even coworkers have asked if I’m okay because I move and react so slowly in the shop.\n- S030 (Worthlessness_Guilt): Most days I feel like a complete fraud who slipped through the hiring process, and I lie awake replaying every tiny oversight at work, convincing myself that I don’t deserve my degree or my job and that everyone would be better off if I weren’t dragging the team down.\n- S031 (Diminished_Thinking): Where I used to be able to zone in on a design problem for hours, now my brain feels like it’s full of static—I reread the same line on a drawing five times, can’t decide something as simple as which tolerance scheme to use, and even picking what to eat for dinner can paralyze me for fifteen minutes.\n- S032 (Death_Suicide): On bad nights, when I’m lying there thinking about how I’ve supposedly ruined everything, I catch myself wishing I just wouldn’t wake up, and I’ve even found myself picturing in detail what it would be like to drive my car off the overpass on my commute, even though I haven’t acted on it.\n▸ Additional Diagnostic Requirements\n- delusions_and_hallucinations_occur_exclusively_during_the_major_depressive_episode: Those thoughts that my boss is secretly building a case against me and the harsh voice telling me I should be punished only started after I slid into this foggy, joyless state last year, and on the rare days when my mood and energy feel closer to normal, that paranoid feeling and the voice pretty much disappear.\n- functional_impairment: Over the past year I’ve gone from being one of the more reliable people on the team to missing internal deadlines, needing coworkers to double-check simple drawings, and turning down almost all social plans because I’m either too exhausted or too wrapped up in worrying about work to function normally outside the job.\n- duration: Ever since a few months after I started the full-time position on this aerospace project—so for a little over a year now—I’ve felt this heavy depression with the guilt, foggy thinking, and scary thoughts about being to blame hanging over me most days.\n\n[Behavioral Guidelines]\n- In the very first turn, only talk about your chief complaint. I tried to cope by working longer hours, but the constant stress eventually wore me down until my brain felt full of static. It is only during those brief moments that I get any kind of break from it.\n\nDoctor: You've mentioned that your symptoms have been steadily worsening over the past fourteen months, but can you clarify if there was ever a two-week period or more during this time where you felt significantly better, or has the depression been persistent without any notable periods of improvement?\n\nPatient: No, there has never been a two-week period where I felt significantly better or back to normal. The heavy fog and guilt have been persistent almost every single day for over a year now. I only get very rare, isolated days where my mood feels slightly closer to normal, but otherwise it has been continuous.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned that your symptoms have been persistent for over a year, but can you confirm if you've had any periods, no matter how brief, where you felt completely back to your normal self, without any depressive symptoms, for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: No, I can definitely confirm I haven't had any two-week periods where I felt completely back to normal. This heavy depression has been constant for over a year now, with no real break from the symptoms. Even on the rare single day where I feel slightly closer to my usual self, it never lasts and I don't feel fully recovered.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any periods of elevated mood, increased energy, or decreased need for sleep that lasted for several days or more at any point in the past fourteen months?\n\nPatient: No, I’ve never experienced anything like that—no periods of high energy, elevated mood, or needing less sleep. It started a little over a year ago, a few months after I transitioned into the full-time aerospace position, and it has been ongoing every day since. I haven't had any periods of recovery or felt like my old self at all during this time.\n\nDoctor: You've described a single, continuous episode of depressive symptoms over the past year, but can you think of any periods, even if brief, where you felt a significant reduction in symptoms for at least a couple of months before they returned, or has it been a steady, unbroken period of depression?\n\nPatient: No, it has been a steady, unbroken period of depression with absolutely no extended relief since it started. I have never had a break of a couple of months where I felt better. At most, I only experience rare, isolated days where I feel slightly closer to normal, but the heavy, foggy feeling always returns immediately.\n\nDoctor: Can you confirm whether you have had any previous episodes of depression before this current one that has lasted over a year, or is this your first experience with such persistent depressive symptoms?\n\nPatient: No, I've never experienced anything like this before. This is the first time in my life that I've ever felt this level of persistent depression and heavy fog.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of at least two months where you felt completely free of depressive symptoms, or has this been the only episode in your life?"

I haven't had any periods of recovery or felt like my old self at all during this time.\n\nDoctor: You've described a single, continuous episode of depressive symptoms over the past year, but can you think of any periods, even if brief, where you felt a significant reduction in symptoms for at least a couple of months before they returned, or has it been a steady, unbroken period of depression?\n\nPatient: No, it has been a steady, unbroken period of depression with absolutely no extended relief since it started. I have never had a break of a couple of months where I felt better. At most, I only experience rare, isolated days where I feel slightly closer to normal, but the heavy, foggy feeling always returns immediately.\n\nDoctor: Can you confirm whether you have had any previous episodes of depression before this current one that has lasted over a year, or is this your first experience with such persistent depressive symptoms?\n\nPatient: No, I've never experienced anything like this before. This is the first time in my life that I've ever felt this level of persistent depression and heavy fog.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of at least two months where you felt completely free of depressive symptoms, or has this been the only episode in your life?\n\nPatient: This is the only episode I've ever experienced in my life. Since it started a little over a year ago, I haven't had any break from the symptoms at all, let alone a two-month period of feeling normal. It has just been one continuous, heavy fog."

Subtypes: Childlike Silliness, Stupor, Catalepsy, Waxy Flexibility, Mutism, Negativism, Posturing, Mannerism, Stereotypy, Agitation, Grimacing, Echolalia, Echopraxia\n[S023] Negative_Symptoms: A state where emotions, motivation, speech, or social interest that should normally be present are diminished or lost.  Subtypes: Diminished Emotional Expression, Avolition, Alogia, Anhedonia, Asociality\n[S024] Depressed_Mood: A state where a sad, empty, or hopeless mood persists.  Subtypes: Depressed Mood, Tearfulness, Sadness, Emptiness, Emotional Numbness\n[S025] Loss_of_Interest: A state where interest or pleasure in previously enjoyed activities is markedly diminished.  Subtypes: Loss of Interest, Social Withdrawal, Loss of Motivation, Anhedonia, Indifference to Achievement\n[S026] Weight_Appetite_Change: Significant increase or decrease in appetite or weight.  Subtypes: Significant Weight Loss, Weight Gain, Decrease in Appetite, Increase in Appetite\n[S027] Sleep_Disturbance: A state where there is a significant change in the quantity or quality of sleep.  Subtypes: Initial Insomnia, Middle Insomnia, Early Morning Awakening, Hypersomnia, Nightmare Disorder, Non-Restorative Sleep\n[S028] Psychomotor_Changes: Observable increase or decrease in psychomotor activity.  Subtypes: Psychomotor Agitation, Psychomotor Retardation (Motor), Psychomotor Retardation (Speech)\n[S029] Fatigue: Persistent fatigue that is not relieved by rest and a marked lack of energy.  Subtypes: Chronic Fatigue, Physical Fatigue, Mental Fatigue, Loss of Energy\n[S030] Worthlessness_Guilt: Feelings of worthlessness or excessive or inappropriate guilt (which may be delusional; not merely self-reproach or guilt about being sick).  Subtypes: Feelings of Worthlessness, Ruminative Guilt, Excessive or Inappropriate Guilt, Delusional Guilt\n[S031] Diminished_Thinking: Diminished ability to think or concentrate, or indecisiveness.  Subtypes: Reduced Concentration, Indecisiveness, Subjective Memory Complaints, Clouded Thinking\n[S032] Death_Suicide: Recurrent thoughts of death, recurrent suicidal ideation, or a suicide attempt. Subtypes: Increased Energy, Hyperactivity, Psychomotor Agitation\n[S035] Inflated_Self_Esteem: Inflated self-esteem or grandiosity (may range from uncritical self-confidence to delusional proportions). Subtypes: Occupational Hyperactivity, Social Hyperactivity, Hypersexuality, Psychomotor Agitation\n[S041] Painful_Consequence_Behavior: Excessive involvement in activities that have a high potential for painful consequences. Subtypes: Fear of Weight Gain, Fear of Specific Foods, Obsession with Weight Gain\n[S046] Body_Image_Weight_Shape_Disturbance: Undue influence of body weight or shape on self-evaluation.  Subtypes: Distorted Body Image, Self-Worth Dependent on Weight\n[S047] Compensatory_Behavior_NonPurging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors to prevent weight gain without purging (direct removal of food).  Subtypes: Fasting, Excessive Exercise\n[S048] Compensatory_Behavior_Purging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors involving the expulsion of food or fluids to prevent weight gain.  Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities. Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge.  Subtypes: Feeling Keyed Up, Physical Tension, Nervousness\n[S059] Concentration_Difficulty: Difficulty concentrating or mind going blank.  Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort.  Subtypes: Neck and Shoulder Tension, Jaw Tension, Generalized Muscle Aches, Chronic Stiffness\n[S062] Specific_Phobic_Fear: Marked fear or anxiety about a specific object or situation that is out of proportion to the actual danger. Subtypes: Derailment or Loose Associations, Tangentiality, Incoherence or Word Salad, Neologisms, Clang Associations\n[S022] Disorganized_Abnormal_Behavior: A state where behavior and motor control are severely disturbed, appearing as movements or reactions inappropriate to the situation.  Subtypes: Childlike Silliness, Stupor, Catalepsy, Waxy Flexibility, Mutism, Negativism, Posturing, Mannerism, Stereotypy, Agitation, Grimacing, Echolalia, Echopraxia\n[S023] Negative_Symptoms: A state where emotions, motivation, speech, or social interest that should normally be present are diminished or lost.  Subtypes: Diminished Emotional Expression, Avolition, Alogia, Anhedonia, Asociality\n[S024] Depressed_Mood: A state where a sad, empty, or hopeless mood persists.  Subtypes: Depressed Mood, Tearfulness, Sadness, Emptiness, Emotional Numbness\n[S025] Loss_of_Interest: A state where interest or pleasure in previously enjoyed activities is markedly diminished.  Subtypes: Loss of Interest, Social Withdrawal, Loss of Motivation, Anhedonia, Indifference to Achievement\n[S026] Weight_Appetite_Change: Significant increase or decrease in appetite or weight.  Subtypes: Significant Weight Loss, Weight Gain, Decrease in Appetite, Increase in Appetite\n[S027] Sleep_Disturbance: A state where there is a significant change in the quantity or quality of sleep.  Subtypes: Initial Insomnia, Middle Insomnia, Early Morning Awakening, Hypersomnia, Nightmare Disorder, Non-Restorative Sleep\n[S028] Psychomotor_Changes: Observable increase or decrease in psychomotor activity.  Subtypes: Psychomotor Agitation, Psychomotor Retardation (Motor), Psychomotor Retardation (Speech)\n[S029] Fatigue: Persistent fatigue that is not relieved by rest and a marked lack of energy.  Subtypes: Chronic Fatigue, Physical Fatigue, Mental Fatigue, Loss of Energy\n[S030] Worthlessness_Guilt: Feelings of worthlessness or excessive or inappropriate guilt (which may be delusional; not merely self-reproach or guilt about being sick).  Subtypes: Feelings of Worthlessness, Ruminative Guilt, Excessive or Inappropriate Guilt, Delusional Guilt\n[S031] Diminished_Thinking: Diminished ability to think or concentrate, or indecisiveness.  Subtypes: Reduced Concentration, Indecisiveness, Subjective Memory Complaints, Clouded Thinking\n[S032] Death_Suicide: Recurrent thoughts of death, recurrent suicidal ideation, or a suicide attempt.  Subtypes: Recurrent Thoughts of Death, Suicidal Ideation, Suicidal Impulse, Specific Plan for Suicide, Suicide Attempt\n[S083] Hopelessness: A feeling of despair or having no hope for the future.  Subtypes: Future Hopelessness, Belief in Impossibility of Improvement, Helpless Resignation, Chronic Pessimism\n[S033] Elevated_Irritable_Mood: A distinct period of abnormally and persistently elevated, expansive, or irritable mood.  Subtypes: Elevated Mood, Irritable Mood, Mood Lability\n[S034] Increased_Activity: Abnormally and persistently increased goal-directed activity or energy.  Subtypes: Increased Energy, Hyperactivity, Psychomotor Agitation\n[S035] Inflated_Self_Esteem: Inflated self-esteem or grandiosity (may range from uncritical self-confidence to delusional proportions).  Subtypes: Inflated Self-Confidence, Overestimation of Abilities, Grandiose Delusions\n[S036] Decreased_Sleep: Decreased need for sleep (e.g., feels rested after only 3 hours of sleep).  Subtypes: Reduced Sleep Duration, Loss of Sleep Need, Indifference to Insomnia\n[S037] Pressured_Speech: More talkative than usual or pressure to keep talking.  Subtypes: Talkativeness, Rapid Speech, Pressured Speech\n[S038] Racing_Thoughts: Flight of ideas or subjective experience that thoughts are racing.  Subtypes: Racing Thoughts, Flooding of Thoughts, Inability to Control Thoughts\n[S039] Manic_Distractibility: Attention is too easily drawn to unimportant or irrelevant external stimuli.  Subtypes: Responsiveness to Extraneous Stimuli, Frequent Task Switching, Inability to Sustain Attention\n[S040] Increased_Goal_Activity: Increase in goal-directed activity (at work, school, socially, or sexually) or purposeless psychomotor agitation.  Subtypes: Occupational Hyperactivity, Social Hyperactivity, Hypersexuality, Psychomotor Agitation\n[S041] Painful_Consequence_Behavior: Excessive involvement in activities that have a high potential for painful consequences.  Subtypes: Unrestrained Buying Sprees, Foolish Business Investments, Sexual Indiscretions, Impulsive Risky Behavior\n[S042] Low_Self_Esteem: A persistent tendency to evaluate oneself as incompetent or worthless; chronically low self-evaluation.  Subtypes: Feelings of Worthlessness, Self-Criticism, Reduced Self-Efficacy, Chronic Sense of Inferiority, Lack of Self-Assurance\n[S043] Restrictive_Intake: Persistent restriction of energy intake relative to requirements, leading to significantly low body weight.  Subtypes: Eating Very Small Amounts, Skipping Meals, Exclusion of Specific Food Groups, Strict Calorie Restriction\n[S044] Low_Weight: A body weight that is significantly below the minimum level of health for age, sex, developmental trajectory, and physical health.  Subtypes: Continuous Weight Loss, Insistence on Low Weight\n[S045] Fear_of_Weight_Gain: Intense fear of gaining weight or of becoming fat, even though at a significantly low weight.  Subtypes: Fear of Weight Gain, Fear of Specific Foods, Obsession with Weight Gain\n[S046] Body_Image_Weight_Shape_Disturbance: Undue influence of body weight or shape on self-evaluation.  Subtypes: Distorted Body Image, Self-Worth Dependent on Weight\n[S047] Compensatory_Behavior_NonPurging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors to prevent weight gain without purging (direct removal of food).  Subtypes: Fasting, Excessive Exercise\n[S048] Compensatory_Behavior_Purging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors involving the expulsion of food or fluids to prevent weight gain.  Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities.  Subtypes: Chronic Worry, Generalized Worry, Catastrophizing, Intolerance of Uncertainty\n[S057] Uncontrollable_Worry: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry.  Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge.  Subtypes: Feeling Keyed Up, Physical Tension, Nervousness\n[S059] Concentration_Difficulty: Difficulty concentrating or mind going blank.  Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort.  Subtypes: Neck and Shoulder Tension, Jaw Tension, Generalized Muscle Aches, Chronic Stiffness\n[S062] Specific_Phobic_Fear: Marked fear or anxiety about a specific object or situation that is out of proportion to the actual danger. (Note: In children, may be expressed by crying, tantrums, freezing, or clinging).  Subtypes: Animal Type, Natural Environment Type, Blood-Injection-Injury Type, Situational Type, Other Type\n[S063] Immediate_Fear/Anxiety_Response: The phobic object or situation almost always provokes immediate fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Anticipatory Anxiety, Immediate Panic Response, Physiological Panic Symptoms, Freezing\n[S064] Phobic_Avoidance_Endure: The phobic object or situation is actively avoided or endured with intense fear or anxiety. Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.  Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others.  Subtypes: Skin Defects, Facial Features, Asymmetry, Muscle Dysmorphia, Specific Body Parts\n[S068] Appearance_Related_Repetitive_Behaviors: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to appearance concerns. Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Internal Avoidance, External Avoidance\n[S071] Trauma_Related_Amnesia: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).  Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world.  Subtypes: Negative Self-Beliefs, Negative Beliefs about Others, Negative Worldview\n[S073] Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame: Persistent, distorted cognitions about the cause or consequences of the traumatic event that lead the individual to blame himself/herself or others.  Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame). Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health.  Subtypes: Risky Behaviors, Substance Abuse, Indisicriminate Sexual Behavior, Self-Harm\n[S079] Hypervigilance: Persistent state of being alert and tense about the surrounding environment.
